ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u1	The next item is the debate on the legislative proposal 35049, Proposal for a law of Members Raemakers and Van Meenen amending the Childcare Act in order to encourage parents to choose between children's centres which may or may not allow children who do not participate in the National Vaccination Programme. I welcome the Lower House members Raemakers and Van Meenen, who are defending the bill on behalf of the Lower House. I had already welcomed the Minister for Social Affairs and Employment and the Secretary of State for Health, Welfare and Sport.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u2	I give the floor to Mr Ganzevoort on behalf of the Group of the Greens/European Free Alliance.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u3	Thank you, Mr President. Let me begin today by expressing my sincere appreciation to colleagues Raemakers and Van Meenen. They have gone the long way of finding the problem, coming up with a solution, translating it into a legislative text, responding adequately to all the questions asked and ultimately convincing a majority of both Chambers, hopefully, in their eyes. 'Cause does the bill make it to the end of next week's vote? Is it a huzzarian piece or a sisyphic work? This seems to be a kind of struggle we have to win or a sport in which we have to perform and we are very quickly in a kind of struggle against each other. But, Mr. Chairman, I just listened very carefully. You did not open the debate on this bill, you opened the debate. That's different. I would like to do that today: debate. That is to say, at least exchange of thoughts, whenever possible consultation and at best arrive at a joint decision. In this debate, as we so often say in motion texts, it is about observing and considering: can we get together what the intentions are, what the problem is, whether the chosen solution works, what side effects can there be, whether it fits within the framework of law and fundamental rights, etc. Let's start with the intentions. My group fully understands where this proposal comes from. Far before corona, the theme of vaccination was important. Too low a vaccination rate means that we are at risk of outbreaks of infectious diseases. In addition, certain groups are particularly vulnerable: the elderly, people with poor health and also, as grandpa, small children. Luckily, major outbreaks with lifelong consequences, such as polio, have been largely eliminated. But history and countries with worse health care teach us how important vaccination is. That is why we also understand that Mr Raemakers presented his own initiative proposal in 2018 in order to "promote parents to choose between children's centres that allow or do not allow children who do not participate in the National Vaccination Programme." Frankly, that sounds very liberal and also sympathetic. The explanatory statement said: there are parents who have doubts about vaccination due to false stories about vaccination and there are parents who are worried about the decreasing vaccination rate. With greater transparency about the admission policy of children's centres, anyone can choose where to place the child. But actually there sounds already a first framing in those sentences that doesn't really help with a pure balancing. It is assumed in advance that parents who do not have their child vaccinated do so because they are guided by false stories from the unscientific social media. That framing gradually shifted a bit. Later, with the written questions, the initiators already put an emphasis on parents who, on ideological and, above all, philosophical grounds, do not allow their child to be vaccinated. Both groups do exist, but there are also regional differences. Even in the big cities there seem to be groups and neighbourhoods where vaccination rates are significantly lower, for example among children with a migration background. In these three groups very different factors play a role: misunderstandings, principles in others, possibly language problems, access to knowledge, cultural expectations. You could say that this liberal starting point does justice to the group of parents who do not inoculate their child in principle. But when it comes to parents with misunderstandings, lack of insight or distance from vaccination care, you would expect completely different solutions, for example more or better information or an active outreach offer. Then you wouldn't so much expect to just let it go, but to approach vaccination in a different way. Apparently, the government was aware of this. The programmes that had already been implemented also started to have an impact in 2019 and 2020. The vaccination rate rose again, to clearly above the critical 90%. It could always be better, but there was a rising line. Despite the pandemic of the last two years, including lockdowns and a huge pressure on care, vaccination rates have fallen only marginally. In other words, the conclusion might be that the large deployment of the vaccination programme is apparently working. That's good news, I'd say. As corona appears to have become more manageable, partly thanks to vaccinations, there is therefore every reason to pursue this policy and, above all, to see how groups with a lower vaccination rate can be better achieved. Therefore, my first question to the initiators and to the government is whether, given the progress of the vaccination rate, there is reason to doubt the policy with which the government is trying to increase that vaccination rate. After all, if that has any effect, the bill may not be necessary. I'd like a response from the petitioners, but also from the government. Anyway, that all seems to be about the vaccination rate, and the question is whether this bill is about that. The bill is about parents' freedom of choice. It is served by greater transparency. That is a laudable goal in itself, but the Council of State rightly said that this is not sufficient as a reason to distinguish between one child and another when registering at a child centre. It does not comply with human and treaty law requirements. This would discriminate against this bill. It would make a distinction without sufficient objective justification. That, of course, was not the intention of the D66 colleagues, a party that is always committed to equal treatment. Therefore, the Council of State's suggestion that the promotion of public health might be sufficient was reluctantly incorporated. I say 'feefully', because it is clear from the further answer that they still think transparency is sufficient. I must also say that they sometimes go into semantics to refute the argument of the Council of State. But they are not actually trying to show that public health is actually served by this proposal and, within the framework of subsidiarity, that this is the lightest means of increasing the vaccination rate. Sure, a vaccination obligation is a heavier one, but better vaccination campaigns are a lighter one, right? I ask the initiators: can you explain that this proposal is the lightest way to increase the vaccination rate effectively?
ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u4	I hear Mr Ganzevoort say: we have to use a different route, a route in which the vaccination rate is simply raised. My question to Mr Ganzevoort is: could he make an estimate of how long such a journey takes, how many years it takes, especially given the past few decades? Secondly, where does he stand with regard to the young children? We are talking about children less than a year old who, over a long series of years, will undoubtedly be the victims of not adopting this law?
ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u5	The latter is an interesting argument, because I have not seen Mr Van Strien's 'undoubtedly' in any of the documents. So I don't know where he gets his lack of doubt, but okay. The question is, can I estimate how long that's gonna take? No, I'm not an expert in that field. What I do know is that the vaccination rate has increased in recent years, and I think that is due to effective policies, apart from the last year, in which the preliminary figures show that, despite corona, there is only a marginal decline.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u6	Well, the numbers have been up for years, but they've been at the same level for a number of years and tend to really stop rising.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u7	Then, in the direction of Mr Van Strien, I must suggest that it might be useful to read the documents and information of the RIVM. They show precisely that the vaccination rate has increased in recent years, 2019 and 2020, for two years in a row. The provisional figures for 2021 indicate a slight decrease of less than 1%. That has to do with corona. That's how I read it in the papers. Perhaps Mr Van Strien can ask the government as well. If it comes with other information, I'd love to hear it, too. If the aim is to increase the vaccination rate, then the question is whether this will help and whether this is the lightest medicine. In that sense, I agree with Mr Van Strien. That should be the question when it comes to restricting fundamental rights, which is also at issue here. I agree with Mr Van Strien that we must also look at effectiveness and feasibility in our deliberations. Will this proposal now lead to greater transparency? Will it lead to an improvement in public health? As far as transparency is concerned, yes, this bill can be increased. It is not a legitimate objective to distinguish between groups of parents, but it is feasible. There are some AVG hobbles to take, but that's it. It's a question of whether it's gonna make a lot of money. It is not an obligation for children's centres, it is merely a possibility. As far as we have understood, there are also children's centres that make use of this possibility and that actually do not register children who have not been vaccinated. Whether it really is going to be used by many more children's centres and whether it really is giving parents more clarity, we will have to wait and see. It may increase transparency, but we are not sure. Then public health. There is a risk that concentrations of unvaccinated children will arise. If we understand correctly, it is scientifically unclear whether this really entails a greater risk of infection outbreaks. But it's not out of the question. Conversely, it is not clear whether it will contribute to the vaccination rate. If public health is the supporting objective, then effectiveness has not been established. That is why I ask the Minister and the initiators whether our reasoning is correct that this is not the purpose of this legislative proposal for public health alone in the technical sense. It is not demonstrably effective. There's one more thing. The legislative proposal seems to focus entirely on the individual interests of parents, for whom there may be more transparency. But when we talk about immunity, it is precisely a collective approach that is important to public health. I remember an election slogan that said, release everyone, but nobody falls. Apart from the stylish figure, you can find quite a bit of it, the thought is very beautiful. On this subject, should this not mean that we are fully committed to public health for everyone, rather than serving the individual interest of self-conscious parents, at the risk of reducing vaccination levels elsewhere? Does this proposal not lead to an even greater division between the health of the wealthy and disadvantaged? I ask the initiators what we can do to really not drop anyone, even when it comes to vaccination of children. There is also another problem. That is the question of whether the Human Rights College is competent after the adoption of this law to give a ruling if there is a complaint. The proposers say no, the government says yes. When this Chamber asked them to come to an understanding, if possible, there was a difference of opinion. I therefore fear that it is not very useful to repeat this here and to ask them about it in this debate. In particular, therefore, I note that the College itself has explicitly replied to this question by the Chamber that it is still competent. Nor will this law make a definitive difference in this respect. Mr. Chairman. In this way we come to discussions and considerations. The vaccination rate is important and should not decrease further. In fact, it should go up. This is particularly true for a number of specific groups of parents. Can the Minister or the Secretary of State tell us more about the vaccination rate within and outside these groups, i.e. not only about the national average, but more specifically about the difference between these different groups? Can the Minister and the Secretary of State also give a little more insight into the specific policy that is being used to reach these groups? If it were possible to increase the vaccination rate right there, we will probably achieve the goal we all want to achieve. I would like to invite the initiators to the same open attitude as I am trying to take. I really understand their concern, but my question is: do they understand the criticism of the Council of State and others about the means chosen by them? As far as the objective is concerned, we may still be able to get very close to each other, but do they understand the discussion about the means? Are they prepared to consider with us and with the government how to promote vaccination rates, especially among groups which are not yet sufficiently achieved? Is it about separating the vaccinated and unvaccinated children or is it about vaccinating as many children as possible so that they can play and learn together? Do they agree with my group that it is precisely liberal to make every effort to reach those groups and not to drop them or deny them access? Mr. Chairman. This might be a moment to talk about dream and act, where laws and practical objections stand in the way, but that poem by Elsschot was about an unfortunate marriage, as Mr Asscher reminded us two years ago. This is something else. This is a bad match between intentions and effect, with a sensitive goal that does not provide enough ground and a rational goal that does not have enough effect. Can we, I ask the initiators, take a step back to see what is really effective in protecting the health of all our children and grandchildren? I look forward very much to an open conversation today and to the answers of the government and the initiators. Thank you.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u13	Thank you, Mr. Ganzevoort. Mrs Gerkens has the floor on behalf of the SP.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u14	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I too would like to express my appreciation and thanks to the authors of this law and their predecessors. They have tried to find a solution to the risk that children will be unintentionally infected with diseases such as measles due to the decrease in participation in the National Vaccination Programme. Mr. Chairman. When I was a child I had a special fear of pricking, and I still suffer from it by the way, because of sitting in the long rows in that in my eyes huge gymnasium with crying children all of whom had to be pricked. You can understand that if my children had to be pricked, I'd always run out of the room and leave it to their father. Nevertheless, I have always taken my vaccinations, even though I'm not a fan of needles. Just after my daughter was born, I met a mother with her severely autistic child in the playground. That mother told me that was because of the vaccination. You understand that I was shocked, because I have a young child there and I see here the result of where this mother was convinced, the vaccination. I immediately dived into it, which is in my nature. Is this a story? I have also come to the conclusion that there is no evidence to support that claim. But you'll understand that if you're sitting there as a young mother in front of someone who's convinced of that, it's still planting a seed. When a few months later my daughter also got sick, who had six months of severe ear infections, who had already had five antibiotic courses a few months later and with eight months of surgery, exactly in the period in which she had to have her inoculation there was no contra-indication..................................................................................................................................... In good consultation, I say, and the daycare was informed. So we were transparent, as the proposers wanted. Mr. Chairman. I tell this story to indicate that I understand very well that young parents may have doubts about giving vaccinations to their child. I am still of the generation who have had the mumps and measles, and I also know how risky and terrible these diseases are. The generation had seen around me the disastrous consequences of diseases such as polio, and so they had no doubts. Worldwide, we try to prevent mortality from these diseases with the vaccination program, and that works. Doubt is allowed, but in all honesty we must conclude that vaccination prevents much suffering. I could slow down that vaccination because the other kids were vaccinated. That is why transparency is important. Mr. Chairman. That brings me to the purpose of this bill. The aim is to increase transparency, thereby increasing the freedom of choice and allowing the daycare centre to refuse children who have not been vaccinated because it does not fit in with their policies. Under this law, almost all unvaccinated children or all unvaccinated children may be refused, except where this vaccination is not possible on medical grounds. The latter, as the proposers say, hardly ever occurs. It's been said. Some daycare centers already pursue this policy, and successfully, others did so but were threatened with a lawsuit. That is why, as the authors say, legislation is needed. Mr. Chairman. That's where the first shoe comes in. Because daycare centres can already refuse children if they have a good argument for that. They are private institutions, which can make demands on the children they allow at daycare as long as they are not discriminatory. There is therefore no need for the law. According to the proposers, the law ensures transparency and improves the freedom of choice. At present, the average waiting time at a daycare facility is about one year. Well, that's for the least popular days. Working parents are lucky to have a place. Outside the Randstad, the range of daycare facilities is also much lower. Often it is one organisation that also covers a large area. The proposers then write that in these cases parents at most have to travel a little further, but more discomfort will not give it. This remark shows little knowledge of the current life of young parents. A daycare center further in the city or in another village quickly gets up half an hour earlier, a huge increase in stress, not to mention the lack of sleep that we already have as a young parent. So there is hardly any freedom of choice now. Then why do the proposers think it will be improved with this proposal, so I ask them. And if so, is freedom of choice also the solution to this problem? Is it up to a daycare center to increase the vaccination rate, or is that an area belonging to the legislator? As the colleague reminded me, the Council of State was also critical of that objective. Insufficient for the legislation, he argued. However, legislation would be possible if, for example, the objective was to promote public health. The applicants then stated that the objective was indeed mentioned in the explanatory memorandum. However, my group reads a possible impact of the legislation in the explanatory memorandum. The legislation could improve parental choice and increase the coverage rate. My group is moving too fast now, because if I don't want to have vaccination, I'm just going to another daycare center where I don't have to. Or I'll go to the hostage shelter. My question to the authors is: do they really think that legislation is necessary to improve public health? If so, what does this legislation contribute to this? What support do they have for this claim? I also ask this because the Council of State insists that a trade-off that infringes the AWGB must be proportionate. As the effect is marginal and therefore the law may not stand up in European courts, my group believes that further justification is necessary. Where is this objective justification which the Council of State points out in its further opinion? Reference to the Vermeij committee is really not relevant here, because it speaks about an obligation for all daycare centres. The proposers deliberately do not choose to do so. The authors say that the law is not necessary because there is now a lack of clarity as to whether or not children should be refused; this law sets a framework. As I said, there is already that possibility, but let us go with the proposers for a moment. The law says that unvaccinated people may refuse or some may refuse when they wish. They leave it to the daycare centers to draw up policies there. The basis for this policy is not given, other than to be transparent. If this does not also lead to legal proceedings, I ask the proposers. How is this law going to prevent the old policy of not converting a requirement from any unvaccinated into 'almost all'? On the basis of which law or policy is this unvaccinated refused? How do we prevent the appearance of discrimination? We know that, for example, the BMR coverage rate for Turkish and Moroccan children is below 90%. Can the proposers indicate how this legislation solves this problem? I would also like to hear the views of the cabinet on this. My group is pleased that medically indicated children do not have to comply with vaccination requirements, but there are few of them, as the proposers themselves said. There are situations where vaccinations may not yet be considered. I'm referring to my daughter. How to deal with cases like this? This legislation will undoubtedly create clusters. The applicants asked RIVM for their opinion. They translate RIVM's statements in such a way that they say that the proposal will not lead to clustering. My group believes that this is not what RIVM says. RIVM says that the policy of refusing non-vaccinated children will reduce the risk of an outbreak only marginally and thus also that the risk of an outbreak of a vaccination-free daycare facility will increase marginally. This means that this legislation will only make a marginal difference in terms of increasing the vaccination rate or the risk of an outbreak. By the way, the outbreak of a vaccination-free daycare facility is much more serious and, de facto, you do not want a daycare centre with unvaccinated children, because an outbreak there is harder than a daycare centre with a mix of vaccinated and unvaccinated children. I would like to repeat the questions to the Secretary of State that the Group of the Greens/European Free Alliance has just asked. I conclude with the pressing question of how we should see this bill in the light of the vaccinations against COVID-19. I look forward to the answers with interest.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u15	Thank you, Mrs Gerkens. Mrs Prins has the floor on behalf of the CDA Group.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u16	Mr. Chairman. In the autumn of 2018, members Raemakers and Van Meenen were concerned about the decreasing vaccination rate for the mumps, measles and rubella, just the bmr vaccinations. It fell more and more below 95%, the limit recommended by the WHO. In 2018, it was 93.6%. These concerns were enhanced by measles being a highly contagious disease, with a reproductive factor of 18. Who could have feared that more than three and a half years later we would be faced with an even more contagious virus, the omicron variant of the COVID-19 virus? The consequences of this infectious virus have therefore been experienced more than ever as a society in recent years. Mr. Chairman. The coronavirus has led to more than ever conversations in society about vaccination and vaccination. Rational, emotional and religious considerations play a role in this and have often led to intense discussions and even demonstrations against a possible vaccination obligation. I would remind you of the discussions we have had here in this Chamber on the introduction of corona tickets in relation to the provisions of our Constitution. Still, around 15% of citizens have not been vaccinated against this virus. Mr. Chairman. In our opinion, you cannot avoid looking at this law in the light of our experiences and decisions about corona and the corona measures. Of course you can't compare apples with pears, says an old saying, but at the same time both are fruit. There are also differences, for example in the subsidiarity and proportionality of the proposed measures. Before I go into this law, I would like to congratulate the two initiators, Raemakers and Van Meenen, for their concern about a falling vaccination rate against possible bmr contamination and for taking action in the form of this legislative proposal. The CDA Group fully agrees with the two initiators on the importance of vaccination. It is a gift, I should like to say: a gift from God that we may have these vaccinations so that we can take responsibility for ourselves and for our fellow human beings in the context of prevention. However, we have to admit that this bill is a bit heavy on our stomachs, from a legal point of view and, secondly, in terms of feasibility and enforcement. The aim of the law is transparency and freedom of choice for parents to choose a child care facility where only vaccinated children, or almost only vaccinated children, are welcome. In other words, private organisations may choose between children who are or are not vaccinated. Here we have to make a comment right away. In normal circumstances, children are not vaccinated against bmr for medical reasons until 14 months. Therefore, every child care facility already has a mix of vaccinated and unvaccinated children. As the RIVM also points out, the main risks of infection for unvaccinated children are not child care but in their social environment. Mr. Chairman. The CDA Group believes with the government, but also with the Verweij committee, author of the report Gijken for each other, that the concern for public health of vaccinated and unvaccinated children is a matter for the government and not for commercial organisations, as also stated in the Public Health Act. The opinion of an individual entrepreneur, an individual employer, should not be decisive for granting or not granting access in relation to the health or being vaccinated by a visitor or an employee. See also the handling of the corona tickets. Not a private entrepreneur, but the government determines the frameworks for everyone. It is not for nothing that Article 22 of the Constitution states that public authorities should take measures to promote public health.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u22	I think that is a point of later order. I have now focused on this subject, all the more so because I wanted to show that we are much more aware of the consequences of a possible vaccination obligation, whether directly or not. That's also what my colleague Ganzevoort said. Action is now being put in place. The CDA Group is looking forward to considering the initiators' considerations of granting powers to a private company by way of derogation from, for example, the corona tickets, thereby infringing the principles of the Constitution. This is all the more true since an approach with more personal and focused communication appears to give the greatest chance of vaccinations. This teaches the research done by Nivel and the AMC on this subject. We have also seen this experience recently in the use of a neighbourhood- and personalised approach by GGD's and GPs on covid vaccination. Mr. Chairman. Then a second observation. The initiators have repeatedly indicated that the purpose of this law is transparency, and the parents' freedom to make informed choices. In the context of proportionality of this law, I quote the initiators: "It is unmistakably disproportionate as the consequences of honoring a wish of the parents of unvaccinated children to always have access to childcare are borne by the parents of the children who have been vaccinated." Apart from the fact that the risks of contagion are present outside childcare rather than within childcare, the RIVM states, in its various opinions, that the objective of transparency and freedom of choice is not sufficiently appropriate and important to serve as a legitimate objective. The use of this law, with the consequence of indirect discrimination, is legitimate if there is objective justification with the necessary medical justification in the light of public health, the Council of State states. The CDA Group noted that the initiators did not indicate with caution until later, i.e. after approval of the proposal in the House of Representatives, that the objective is, in the second instance, the protection of public health. What is the reason why this addition in the field of public health has initially been so explicitly rejected, we ask the initiators. Mr. Chairman. Then another observation. It appears that there are already several childcare centres applying vaccination policies. Only vaccinated children are welcome there. The initiators indicate that this situation does exist now, but that there is still a risk that a parent who does not endorse this view may have recourse to the General Equal Treatment Act. Therefore, the initiators have provided for an addition to the Childcare Act, namely that the holder of a children's centre may provide that only or almost exclusively staff are employed in any form and children are admitted who can be shown to participate in the vaccination programme referred to in Article 6b, first paragraph of the Public Health Act, in so far as medical reasons are not detectable. The initiators have indicated that they have added this so-called lex specialise, given the provisions of the General Law on Equal Treatment. In this way, the initiators write, there would no longer be room for further consideration in specific cases by the judge or by the Human Rights College. In reply to a question from the First Chamber, the chairman of this college also indicated that despite this additional article, the college is still competent. I quote: "The activities of children's centres as employers respectively (in the case of vaccination as a job requirement for current/future workers) and as a provider of goods and services (in the case of vaccination as an admission requirement for children) fall within the scope of equal treatment legislation." How do the initiators view this position of the college? Mr. Chairman. The initiators support their proposal, including in answering various questions from this Chamber, with Article 24 of the UN Children's Rights Committee, which states that the government has a duty to inform parents of factors affecting the health of the child and to ensure that parents can make informed choices. We have difficulty in finding the relationship between this article and the bill, which allows childcare centres to decide whether or not they allow unvaccinated children. Does this not mean that the government must be very active in communicating on the advantages and disadvantages of vaccination, activities such as those which have now been intensively taken up by the government? Please give an explanation from the initiators. As a CDA group, we note that there is some lightness about feasibility and enforcement. Children at a young age are regularly vaccinated. So every child in a child care facility has its own puncture interval, so to speak. The initiators see no objection in this; that can also be done with a calendar. In addition, that is the idea, a child care company chooses for it itself and therefore takes the administration for granted. However, the CDA Group would like to point out that this is very personal information, which each organisation has to deal with continuously. It seems to us to be desirable to monitor this. We do not have such elements in the legislative proposal. Mr. Chairman. In conclusion, the CDA Group strongly supports the promotion of vaccinations. For us, the question remains whether this legislative proposal is the right way, especially in the light of legitimacy, proportionality and subsidiarity. The vaccination rate for bmr is at a reasonable to good level. We call on the government to make a firm commitment to adequate and personal communication. This is an approach that works in our country and which, in our opinion, is justified, given the vaccination rate, which fortunately is much higher than the vaccination rate for COVID-19. We are curious about the answers to our questions.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u23	Thank you, Mrs. Prince. I call Mr Van der Voort on behalf of the D66 Group.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u24	Mr. Chairman. I would like to congratulate the initiators on the initiative proposal and also on the patience they have shown to come to a conclusion. The D66 Group therefore welcomes the fact that today we can debate the legislative proposal on the option of children's centres on the basis of participation in the National Vaccination Programme. Vaccination has been in extraordinary interest over the past two years. This is, of course, about vaccination against the SARS-CoV-2 virus, but let it be clear before this debate, as I pointed out in my interruption, that this initiative legislative proposal is about the National Vaccination Programme. The so-called corona vaccination is not part of that. Whether it will be part of it in the future is not relevant either. The proposal for an initiative concerns the principle of choice whether unvaccinated children may be refused by childcare centres. Our group therefore asks the initiators whether they can explain once again the reasons for this initiative proposal and how it relates to the reasons that opponents have to refuse to do so. Mr. Chairman. Our group believes it is important to stress that the proceeds of vaccinations are primarily for the benefit of the vaccinees themselves. The effects of vaccination programmes are high worldwide and the National vaccination programme is also successful in the Netherlands. Research shows that vaccinations worldwide have prevented at least 30 million dead children in low and middle income countries between 2000 and 2019. I'm gonna give you a few more figures on how big the effects of vaccination are. Since 1903, mortality rates of infectious diseases from the National vaccination programme in the Netherlands have decreased by almost half. Per 10,000 inhabitants this decreased from 156 deceased in the year 1903 to 84 in 2012. The average number of years of life lost to these infections was 3.8 years for children born in 1903, 0.59 years for children born in 1952 and only 0.16 years for children born in 1992. That's a strong reduction in early mortality from vaccinations. We're talking polio, tetanus, whooping cough, diphtheria, measles, ruffled dog and mumps. The National Vaccination Programme has thus prevented between 6,000 and 12,000 deaths among children and young adults in the Netherlands, born between 1953 and 1992. Let's just say the generation is the way we are here. That's hundreds of thousands of years of life. Statistically, one of us would not have been here if there had been no vaccinations such as those included in the National vaccination programme. And some of us would suffer from complications, sometimes lifelong, like polio, without these vaccinations. The figures I mention here relate to the seven vaccines against the classic childhood diseases I have just mentioned. Meanwhile, the current National vaccination programme protects with eight injections against twelve infectious diseases. Fortunately, vaccination readiness has been very high among the population for a long time, but a few years ago it was somewhat declining. This bill came into being in the time when there was unrest in society about the relatively low vaccination rate. Parents were therefore concerned about whether their child was safe in the daycare center. A choice to take your child to a childcare center where only vaccinated children are offers some rest for these parents. How is it that vaccination has come into a less good light? Traditionally there are religious considerations not to give vaccinations, but there are also parents who are reluctant from, for example, an anthroposophical mindset. Scientific insights in some parents are no longer self-convincing to arrive at a choice of vaccination. The weighting of pros and cons in parents can lead to a choice not to have their child vaccinated. Fortunately, for an unvaccinated individual, there is protection in a population with a high vaccination rate: group immunity. This is an additional effect of vaccination experienced by unvaccinated individuals. Vaccinates with absent or reduced immune response also benefit from this. In the children's centers we are talking about, some children are not yet fully vaccinated because of their age. It is therefore very important for them to be together with as many vaccinated children as possible. An individual consideration of parents leading to a choice not to participate in the National vaccination programme is therefore not in the interests of society and in this case the children in the childcare centres. At the same time, an unvaccinated child itself has no interest in a lower vaccination rate in society, an apparent contradiction. We therefore ask the cabinet what it intends to do to keep the vaccination rate high; it has been discussed before in the debate. This protects both vaccinated and unvaccinated children? Mr. Chairman. This debate is not about convincing that vaccinations are useful. Even the opponents of this bill, I suppose, will not dispute the figures mentioned. The point is that children who, by choosing their parents, knowingly maintain a naive immune system for the pathogens mentioned in the National vaccination programme can be refused by childcare centres. In doing so, it is important to realise that this initiative proposal does not create a vaccination obligation, not even an indirect one. In fact, the initiators' expectations are that there will be sufficient room in nursery homes or host families that will not apply the refusal. Can the initiators provide further support for this? The bill offers parental freedom of choice and transparency about vaccination status. RIVM recalls that almost half of unvaccinated children are already going to a childcare facility where the vaccination rate is less than 80%. The RIVM report estimates that there is no reason to believe that this bill will increase the risk in the children's centres where unvaccinated children are being taken care of. According to the RIVM report, the other half of the unvaccinated children go to a site where the vaccination rate is high. The bill states that these locations can refuse these children but should not refuse. It is therefore not an obligation of these children's centres but a choice. This makes it clear to parents that their child does not come into contact with children who are kept out of the vaccination programme. This leads to choice for the parents. Is it legitimate to refuse access to a childcare centre as a result of the choice not to vaccinate? The D66 Group believes that the initiators have made this plausible. The European Court of Human Rights has ruled on a Czech vaccination obligation and the subsequent admission or refusal of a nursery. The Court considered that to be fair. This is an important signal, as the present legislative proposal remains well within the limits of that judgment. Mr. Chairman. The legal and moral aspects that have so far been discussed in the House of Representatives and the written questions are relevant and need to be addressed. Our group takes the view that vaccination issues are mainly concerned with the medical effects of vaccination, both for the individual and for society. From that position, in which health is valued, we support this initiative legislative proposal. The D66 Group has wondered whether the balance of interests between parents and children with and without vaccination by the initiators has been sufficient. Our group thinks so. We live in a society and depend on how we take care of each other and protect each other. A high vaccination rate is appropriate. Freedom of choice for parents to send their child to a childcare centre where the other children have been vaccinated is also appropriate.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u134	Thank you, Mr President. I would like to welcome the initiators and the two directors to this Chamber and wish them every success. It is for you all four the first debate in the First Chamber. Good luck! Mr. Chairman. It has been two years since this initiative law was presented to this Chamber for consideration. As a result, the result of the legislative proposal may have been somewhat out of the picture. In the past year we have all dealt with this other viral threat to public health, the COVID-19 virus. The world population has once again experienced what a huge impact a serious infectious disease has on all of our lives. We had, at least in most countries, forgotten what that is like and therefore became particularly unchalant when it comes to maintaining a vaccination rate that is necessary to avoid giving viruses a chance. Mr. Chairman. It is a problem when more and more people refrain from vaccination for whatever reason, because then the so-called group immunity is broken down. As a result, an infectious disease can flare up again, as in 2019, when a number of children were infected with a possible measles virus imported from abroad in a daycare center in The Hague. Recently, the WHO reported that vaccination programmes have reduced the number of measles deaths worldwide by 94% over the last 20 years: from over 1 million to 60,000 deaths per year. Those 60,000 are still too many, of course, because it will only be your child. Unfortunately, facts do not always speak for themselves anymore at this time, because more and more people think that vaccination should be called into question. So I understand very well that there are parents who are concerned about the health of their children, when the measles virus in particular is gaining ground again. Mr. Chairman. The VVD Group believes that these parents' concerns should be taken seriously. In this context, my group very much appreciates the initiative proposal, which allows parents to opt for childcare where only participants in the National Vaccination Programme are allowed. But whether this is enough to prevent a measles outbreak in the child center in question is still the question. Many of you have already mentioned this today. Should the government no longer do so, is my group's question. First of all, we believe that the government should be much clearer to citizens about the importance of vaccination and, above all, about the consequences of non-vaccinating. Then why should you have your child vaccinated when a disease in the Netherlands is virtually non-existent? I would like a response from the Secretary of State to the government's not exactly appealing communication style in this matter. Vaccination in the Netherlands is voluntary, but to what extent is infectious disease control free of obligation for the government? The WHO report I have just referred to is being continued with the call to governments to bring the vaccination rate to the highest possible level and to actively combat the falling vaccination trend. I would like a response from the Secretary of State on how the government is going to respond to this call. What objectives has the government set? And what is the government going to do to bring public communication on the National Vaccination Programme into line with this? Mr. Chairman. The measles virus is highly contagious. To control this virus, a high vaccination rate is necessary. In the Netherlands the vaccination rate has been well below the critical lower limit of 95% indicated by the WHO. It is particularly risky for children under 14 months of age who are not yet able to participate in the National Vaccination Programme, but who may be in contact with infected people at child care. It is therefore understandable that parents of these very young children insist on measures at a low vaccination rate. They want to make sure that their child is taken care of in the safest possible environment. According to the VVD Group, therefore, a legislative proposal which clearly states that child care measures can be taken in case of insufficient vaccination rates is more appropriate. This initiative proposal states that parents can make an informed choice between child centres that allow or do not allow children who participate in the National Vaccination Programme. According to the initiators, the crux of the initiative proposal is to remove the freedom of choice and legal uncertainty for childcare providers wishing to pursue a refusal policy. In principle, private parties are free to set conditions for access to services and services. An argument in that context is then the duty of care. Childcare organisations should ensure a safe and healthy environment for the children and for the workers. The potential disadvantage of access policy is to limit the freedom of choice and the risk of unfair exclusion. In the context of this proposal, the question arises as to whether, in the context of the obligation of care, you can infringe physical integrity. For access you have to participate in the National vaccination programme, and vaccination is an invasive medical operation. In its opinion on Ethics and Legal Considerations COVID-19 vaccination of February 2021, I would like to add that the assessment of whether there is a restriction in these fundamental rights depends on whether alternatives are being offered. The alternative to this is the option for child centres that do not pursue a policy of refusal, the initiators said. The VVD Group supports the initiators' argument that this proposal does not exclude unvaccinated children from childcare. The initiators therefore expect that only part of the child centres will have a policy of refusal. In this context, however, pediatricians warn of the risk of raising infectious diseases in children in child centres who do not have a policy of refusal, the so-called cluster risk. In a letter dated 3 September 2021, this care of pediatricians is taken away by the RIVM, we interpret it in any case. I quote: 'The risk of raising infectious diseases in children in areas where child centres restrict access for unvaccinated children will be equal to that of other areas'. So there seems to be little obstacle to this initiative law, at least as far as this argument is concerned, if I am not left with the question of whether this initiative proposal is justified in removing parents' concerns. In the reply, the initiators wrote: "A child centre's policy regarding the admission of unvaccinated children is clearly a factor that can affect the health of a child staying at a child centre." Is that so? In the same letter from the RIVM we read: "The risk of infection in children is not directly affected by the access restriction. This means that the risk of infection remains the same in the children and adults." I would like a response from the initiators to this opinion of the RIVM. If the risk of contamination in child centres with access policies remains the same as in child centres without access policies, what does that mean for the legitimate purpose of this initiative law? In the reply, the initiators indicate that the legislative proposal can contribute to increasing the vaccination rate. RIVM says in an annex to the aforementioned letter: "The only thing that really helps is measures aimed at more vaccinations." Looking at the intervention ladder of the Nuffield Council on Bioethics, access limitation is a next step to move people into vaccination, when advising and persuaded have insufficient effect. However, the initiators do not opt for a general access restriction but for a partial access restriction. A general access restriction is expected to be more effective than the present differentiated proposal. However, we read in the documents that both the initiators and the government believe that the regulation of general conditional access is rather complex. In that case, it is stated that in the interest of the child, in the fight against a declining vaccination rate, the government has a wide margin of freedom with regard to the imposition of vaccinations. Now, of course, access limitation, which we are talking about today, is a much less far-reaching step than a vaccination obligation. In the same ruling, the European Court of Human Rights also states that the exclusion of unvaccinated children in childcare is not inadmissible, provided that the child is not deprived of the right to education. But that only happens in a later phase of life. The European Court of Human Rights qualifies access restrictions as a protection measure. As far as proportionality is concerned, the European Court of Human Rights considers that measles vaccination is evidence of a substantial improvement in public health. So that is also what the WHO reports I have just indicated show.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u197	Mr President, you have the opportunity to just put ten minutes on the plate. Thank you for that. Mr. Chairman. Let me begin by expressing my respect for the perseverance of the initiators. Not only have they submitted a bill four years ago, but they have also defyed everything and everyone. After all, all the advisory bodies that could be of any relevance have now made their point. When I look over that battlefield, all the signals are pretty much red. However, four years later, we are now in a debate with the proposers. But this Chamber is not there to reward the virtue of perseverance, but to judge the object of that perseverance, namely the law, on the well-known aspects of legality, feasibility and enforcement. In addition, this Chamber also feels a special responsibility when legislation is shattered with the Constitution. The present law seeks to justify an infringement of the Constitution, at least on the AWGB resulting from Article 1 of the Constitution. It is precisely then that it is right to lay down the proposal along the bar of proportionality, subsidiarity and also effectiveness. Let us now turn to the subject and first to the issue of proportionality. If we have learned anything in recent times, it is that we should not force people to vaccinate and that we should avoid permanently excluding people from participation in society. Of course, that also applies to children, even when they are very young. There is a very difficult point in this law, because although the initiators argue that the aim of the bill is to achieve transparency in the market for children's centres, it is actually about providing legal space for holders of children's centres to refuse non-vaccinated children. This leads to a form of compulsion, especially in a society in which everyone is supposed to participate in the labour market. Parents who depend on child care may feel forced to have vaccination. This also leads to a form of exclusion. If you haven't been vaccinated, you can be kicked out. Now it is about childcare, but the step to other places where children meet, such as education, is small if we cross this line. Does the government see this danger? After all, many childcare centres are located in schools where primary school children are also taught during the day. All this because of something that played a few years ago, when it turned out that the vaccination rate had deteriorated slightly. The figures have already been mentioned enough today. In any case, this will lead to far-reaching steps in this legislative proposal, with the necessary questions still to be asked. These are, for example, questions relating to the principle of subsidiarity, the starting point being that the least radical means of achieving a particular objective must be used. If the objective is to lay a legal ground under the jurisdiction of childcare centres, the slightest means is to do nothing. After all, the Council of State on several sides argued that childcare centres are private enterprises. A quote from the Council of State, especially for Mr Van Strien and Mrs De Bruijn-Wezeman: "As such, they do not need explicit legal powers to formulate an admission policy to their services. Even without the proposed legislative changes, childcare centres have a margin of freedom to refuse children." Just to be sure, I'm gonna ask you if this statement by the Council of State is correct. That is what I am asking both the proposers and the government and the secretaries of state. I assume the answer will be affirmative. It is striking that the same reasoning is used by the Health Council in an opinion on the use of corona vaccination certificates by private institutions and companies. Private parties have the freedom to impose a vaccination certificate as a condition for access to services and facilities. This also applies to access to children's centres, as these are often private companies. Do the petitioners agree with the Health Council that this was true for corona vaccinations? If not, have they acted against this in the legislative processes around covid? If so, why is there a different regime now? In other words, why this bill? I ask this all the more because we are dealing with an important side effect, namely that parents feel more compelled to have their children vaccinated. I didn't make that up, but the initiators say so themselves. They expect that, after the entry into force of this bill, a significant proportion of children's centres will adopt a policy of differentiation based on vaccination status for both staff and children. The proposers see as a positive result of this bill that it may be a push for doubting parents to have the children vaccinated. That is not the primary objective, but apparently it is an objective. Do the proposers agree with me that this so-called side effect can be compelling, or at least seen as compelling?

It is not for nothing that this Chamber has asked for additional information from the Council of State on exclusion from childcare centres of children and workers who have not been vaccinated out of religious or philosophical beliefs. In its reply, the Council of State pointed out the scope available through a special law, the lex specialis, which would allow an exception to the prohibition of indirect discrimination. The proposers have embraced this way out, you might say. This gave rise to further questions to the Human Rights College, because, according to the Council of State, as a result of such a lex specialization, there would no longer be room for a different balance of interest by the judge or the Human Rights College in concrete cases. However, the Human Rights College argues that the proposed initiative law does not change its competence to conduct an individual review. The College shall retain the power to examine and assess "whether the decision/behaviour of the relevant employer or provider of goods or services in the particular case is actually covered by the exception to the prohibition of distinction and is therefore justified'. There is therefore room between the interpretation of the Council of State and the College. What is the reaction of the proposers to this room? And how does the government weigh this discrepancy? Do the proposers and the government agree with the SGP Group that this will not lead to the desired legal clarity? Mr. Chairman. Another important point is that of making an indirect distinction on the basis of religion and belief. That is not, by definition, prohibited, but it must serve a legitimate purpose. According to the Council of State, the objective of transparency is not sufficient. However, the initiators make it clear that one wants to distinguish on the basis of vaccination status with the secondary aim of protecting the health of children and therefore public health in general. This would be a legitimate objective and would justify an indirect distinction on the basis of religion or belief in the AWGB. Well, fortunately, we still have an RIVM, an institute that has in recent years set out the direction of all kinds of measures, which have, by the way, been supported by the proposers. RIVM clearly states that this bill will in no way contribute to improving the safety of children's centres. The introduction of access restrictions does not affect the risk of contagion in children or adults; it has just been exchanged in a small debate between Mrs Vos and one of the other speakers. After all, the composition of the group at children's centres is too diverse for the possibility that group immunity by vaccination could play a relevant role. RIVM indicates that group immunity within the daycare facility does not play a role, but is only relevant at population level, either national, regional or local. With the RIVM's replies, the second basis under this bill would be to abolish the exception to indirect discrimination on the basis of religion or belief, namely the protection of public health. Mr. Chairman. I come to some conclusions and ask the proposers and the government to reflect on them. The approach of the bill is noble: transparency for parents as regards choosing a children's center and protecting children's health. However, the bill starts with the parents' right to make an informed choice and ends with a hidden form of vaccination urge, leaving less and less room for the parents' own informed choice. Two. If the grounds for protecting public health fall under this bill, it is clear that the indirect distinction based on religion or belief is not justified. In other words, the Council of State has confirmed this. Third. Similarly, the proposal does not solve the risk for children's centres to become involved in long-term and costly legal proceedings because of a policy of refusal of unvaccinated children. The Human Rights College makes it very clear that in any case it remains competent to examine specific cases.

Care for their child also includes the prevention of diseases. Older unvaccinated children or employees may infect younger children who may not yet be vaccinated. For example, a child aged 10 months can be infected with measles by an unvaccinated child of one and a half months, because the vaccine is only given from the age of about 12 months. Parents and employees are free not to cooperate with the request to provide vaccination status anonymously to third parties. They are in no way forced to do so. Our group supports this proposal in full. Thank you.

There's been a lot of work in it. It is good to see that a young Member of Parliament, I can say, does not avoid the complicated process of an initiative law. I would also like to thank the staff who contributed to this law, even if only because they would end up in the Acts of the First Chamber. These are Huub Linthorst, Sabeth de Boer, Koen Sijtsema, Levi Bakker and Anne-Lise Olsthoorn. Mr. Chairman. I listened carefully, starting with Mr Ganzevoort's contribution. He referred for a moment to others who have also done so, that he is not only here as a Member of Parliament, but also as a Grandpa. I am. I don't want to leave that out. We might be able to exchange photos later. But of course it gives an extra dimension, we should not hide that. This is ultimately about the interests of all children in the country, including our children. It makes me feel a little weird. You may have seen me staggering to the pulpit. That might be a little overzealous. If you see my age, you might think it's polio. It's not, but I've been there. One of you mentioned that, too. That was one of those moments. I also remember that I was in class with children who were hit by polio and how serious that was. I also remember what that meant in the end for what we all managed to achieve in this country, that whole National Vaccination Programme. I'll come back to that later. Of course, Mr Van der Voort has already shown how far-reaching that has been. Mr. Chairman. There's been a lot of history preceding this bill. October 3rd is always a beautiful day for me as a Leader anyway. I see Mr. Schalk nodding; he knows about it. That was also the case in 2018. Then my colleague Rens Raemakers filed this bill. The coronavirus did not exist yet, but there were widespread concerns about the vaccination rate regarding some very dangerous childhood diseases, such as polio and measles. In August of that year an online petition was launched that would eventually be signed by more than 52,000 Dutch. The petition asked the government to make vaccinations mandatory for access to childcare. Mr Raemakers didn't want to go that far. However, he felt that parents should be able to opt for a children's center in which only vaccinated children would be admitted. The cabinet responded to this in December 2018 with the setting up of the Childcare and Vaccination Committee, also known as the Vermeij Committee. It will, of course, be discussed in detail later in my speech. On 1 July 2019 she published her report. The committee advocated a law which would oblige parents who would like to take their child to a childcare centre to participate in the National vaccination programme if the vaccination rate had fallen below a critical limit. This opinion came at a time when the written examination of our legislative proposal was almost complete. However, the committee's report could still be included in the oral hearing in the Lower House. This led to a proposal for an amendment from the SP Group, which was intended to be in line with that opinion of the Committee on Childcare and Vaccination. Fortunately, however, the author of the amendment finally recognised that the amendment essentially provided for the complete replacement of the legislative proposal with a new, quite different legislative proposal, and that this would not, in that sense, lead to any careful legislative procedure. Later, in December 2019, the Cabinet asked the Advisory Section of the Council of State for information on the opinion of the Committee on Childcare and Vaccination. It was released in March 2020. That was also sent to your Chamber by the Cabinet. It was possible to conclude from this information that there are still some shortcomings in the committee's proposal. Meanwhile, today's bill was adopted by the House of Representatives on 18 February 2020. Then, a month later, the corona virus struck. Of course, this has delayed the examination of the bill in this Chamber, but I do appreciate it very much, and I would like to say here that it has not prevented you, in addition to all the priority that had to be given to corona-related legislative proposals, from dealing with this bill. First, you asked for information from the Advisory Section of the Council of State. Two rounds of written questions were then held. In the end, a position of the College of Human Rights has also been requested from your Chamber. Finally, as initiators, we also asked for an opinion from the RIVM on the so-called cluster risk. In the meantime, your Chamber also has access to it. That is why we believe that the treatment has been extremely careful. In that spirit, today, I want to answer the questions I have been asked to the best of my ability. I am happy to do so in the attitude shown by Mr Ganzevoort: a nice open debate with each other. That's how I'm always in it. Mr. Chairman. To answer the questions of the Chamber properly, I have several cubes, as it is called in the House of Representatives. I don't know if that's what it's called in this environment, but you know what I mean. Firstly, I would like to talk about the content and objectives of the legislative proposal and the principle of subsidiarity. The second block deals with the legal framework. Three. The principle of proportionality. Fourthly, the social debate and fifthly, a number of separate questions. Mr. Chairman. The primary objective of the bill is to facilitate an informed choice for parents who want to have their child taken into a children's center. Not because of an economic-liberal concern about the functioning of the market, but because of one specific higher objective, namely that children can grow up as healthy as possible, so free from............................................................................................................................................................................................................ This right can be achieved by inoculating as many children as possible against dangerous infectious diseases against which safe and effective vaccinations exist. This is important not only for children who are actually vaccinated under the National Vaccination Programme, but through the so-called group immunity for all. That is what I am saying to Mr Ganzevoort. It also concerns children under 14 months of age who are not yet able to participate in the National vaccination programme. It is therefore also a question of public health. To the question of the Labour Party, I can answer that this law has as its primary objective to promote the informed freedom of choice of parents and as a secondary objective to promote public health. You will understand that I will come back to that in detail. For a long time we could rely in the Netherlands on a relatively high level of vaccination against the relevant diseases, but since 2014 it started to decrease. In 2020, RIVM reported that vaccination rates had increased slightly for the first time in five years. With regard to measles, it was then 93.6% for 14 months of infants and 89.7% for 9 years of the second shot. RIVM still expects an outbreak of these dangerous childhood diseases on average every ten years. However, it has recently become known that the vaccination rate for the BMR vaccination of mumps, measles, rubella and for the dktp vaccination of diphtheria, whooping cough, tetanus and polio. The number of unvaccinated children increases above 8% at dktp and at the bmr the number is above 6%. As a result of this decrease, we fall even further below the 95% limit of group immunity and thus the risk of outbreaks of very dangerous and contagious diseases such as measles becomes more real. In order to protect our children, it remains important to increase the vaccination rate. Various measures are conceivable in this respect. The report by the Committee on Childcare and Vaccination gives some insight into this. It combines a mandatory character with a certain degree of vaccination.

Mr. Chairman. The objective is therefore extended to public health in a broad sense. As mentioned earlier, its protection is not something that can be promoted by a single law or measure. A European legal framework on vaccinations in relation to childcare can be found in the opinion of the European Court of Human Rights on the appeal of Mr Vavricka and five other Czech citizens. This was a general duty in the Czech Republic. By the way, I would like to add that this is the next block.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u323	Is this the block of legal frameworks?
ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u324	Yes. This was a general legal obligation to vaccinate children against nine diseases. That's similar to our national vaccination program. Compliance with this obligation cannot be physically enforced in the Czech Republic. However, parents who fail to comply with this obligation without good reason may be fined once. Unvaccinated children are not accepted in what we call 'child centres' here. So that goes far beyond the current legislative proposal. On 8 April last year, the Court ruled that this legislation did not infringe the European Convention for the Protection of Human Rights. The Court gives an overview of a series of international conventions and other legal instruments, from which a positive commitment to public trade can be derived, especially if the vaccination rate is lower than that required for group immunity, which is currently the case in the Netherlands. The Court stresses that all decisions relating to children must always take priority over their interests. The Court states that the aim must be to protect every child from serious illness, vaccination or group immunity. The Court therefore considered that the Czech health policy was in line with the interests of the children to which it was addressed. According to the Court, the Czech legislation was justified by the same two legitimate objectives underlying the present legislative proposal: the protection of the health and rights of others. The Czech case was quite different from that of the present bill. Our proposal is not about a vaccination obligation in any form whatsoever. According to the Court, the Czech Republic remained within the broad margin of discretion conferred by the Treaty on Member States, which implies that States can opt for more or less far-reaching measures. We have deliberately chosen the least radical proposal that we could come up with: to facilitate a choice, not only to have your children vaccinated or not, but all parents already have that choice, and that remains so, but also to allow your children to be taken care of in a children's center that allows only or almost only vaccinated children. There is therefore no coercion and exclusion as suggested here by the SGP. I shall drop a significant silence here because I do not know whether Mr Schalk wants to come back to that.

I say this with some emphasis, particularly with regard to freedom of religion, because it was previously suggested that the measure contained in the bill conflicts with the fundamental right of freedom of religion or belief. The bill does not in any way prevent people from confessing or expressing their religion in worship or in keeping commandments and regulations. The bill only concerns the fundamental right to equal treatment. In the legal framework relevant to the assessment of the legislative proposal, a central place is taken by our General Law on Equal Treatment. We are also closely following the system of the directive. Part of this is the difference between direct and indirect distinction, as has already been mentioned here. Direct distinction is never allowed, except for a few specific exceptions. Indirect distinction may be justified where there is a legitimate purpose and the means of achieving that objective are appropriate and necessary. The present legislative proposal concerns indirect discrimination on the basis of religion or belief. Of course, everyone is free to take the view that indirect discrimination on the basis of religion should never be justified, but that is not the starting point of the EU directive and of our own General Law on equal treatment. Indirect distinction can be justified. This can be done in two ways: by a specific legal provision or by a judge's ruling. Mr. Chairman. ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u409	Mr. Chairman. It's all still about that Human Rights College. This creates the possibility for parents to opt for a children's center as safe as possible. This concerns the interests of children, of parents, of holders of a children's centre and of their staff. I would like to point out that children's interests are always the first consideration. In this I follow the European Court of Human Rights in the judgment I mentioned earlier in the Czech case. It is also about the interests of everyone who lives in the Netherlands. These interests are served by the bill insofar as they lead to an increase in the vaccination rate. This can be particularly important in those regions where vaccination rates are below 95%. For children, this legislative proposal is important because it can lead, much more than is currently the case, to children's centres where only children participating in the National Vaccination Programme are admitted. With this law, we want to make a positive contribution to the vaccination rate. With this we serve the interests of all children. After all, a higher vaccination rate reduces the risk for each individual child. The PvdA's question is whether there is a greater risk of infection in children's centres that do not have a specific authorisation policy due to a concentration of unvaccinated children. In view of the questions raised in previous written rounds, we have, as you have heard, requested an opinion from the RIVM. This shows that in the event of an outbreak, the virus must be brought into the children's centre. For the infection risk there is only one dominant factor and that is the vaccination rate in the municipality or region where the children's center is located. If it's above 95%, there's no big deal. If it's lower, it gets worrying. RIVM therefore states, and I quote: 'The risk of raising infectious diseases in children in areas where children's centres restrict access to unvaccinated children will be equal to that in other areas'. Mr. Chairman. Parents who do want their child to participate in the National Vaccination Programme contribute both to the safety and health of their own children and to the unvaccinated children. Their interests will have to weigh heavily. It is understandable that they want to opt for a children's centre that allows only vaccinated children, especially in regions with a low vaccination rate. It is therefore important to mention, also in view of the Christian Union's remark, that this law does not, of course, provide children with full safety against dangerous infectious diseases. It does allow parents to do everything possible in the interests of their child's health. On the question of the VVD, the SGP and GroenLinks on the effectiveness of the law, given the RIVM's assertion that an admission policy does not have a direct impact on the risk of infection, I note the following. We believe that the law as a whole has an effect on the health of children at micro level, by the one child who, by virtue of this law, participates in the National vaccination programme, but also at macro level by contributing to the vaccination rate. This is also in line with the opinion of the Council of State of 17 June 2020, which states that, with all the effects of the refusal by childcare centres of unincorporated children and workers on public health in the broad sense and on children's health in childcare, it is expected to be positive although limited. That is exactly our view. This is gonna do something. Of course you'd want more, but it does something. And then we can't let it go. Mr. Chairman. In addition, as I said, we have never been able to guarantee full safety in relation to infectious diseases.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u497	Thank you very much for allowing us, as directors responsible, to express the opinion of the cabinet on this initiative legislative proposal.
