ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u498	Can you always tell at the end of the blocks that it's the end of a block? Then we know we can interrupt.
ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u499	Yes. I'm trying to answer your question as much as I can. The cabinet position deals with the opinion of the Cabinet, the Council of State, the purpose of the initiative legislative proposal, the Human Rights College, the Vermeij Committee and the implementation. Then there are probably four or five specific questions left. The Secretary of State will then address everything related to vaccination. That's his job. That is the state of affairs, the RIVM, how the vaccination in the Netherlands is going. He also has a whole range of other questions. Then, Mr President, on this initiative proposal. It aims to create transparency in the childcare market. You all know this... so that parents can make a well informed choice. A lot of parallels have already been drawn with children and grandchildren. I, too, have four. They are now well off childcare. That makes me, like many of you, an experienced expert. I'm also a physicist, by the way, but we're not going to talk about statistics tonight. I think that's just confusing. The aim of the proposal is also to provide childcare centres with transparency on the legal admissibility of a refusal policy. Following the information provided by the Council of State, the initiators added a secondary objective of public health protection. Those aims of the initiators are very sympathetic to us. Raising the vaccination rate is, of course, very important. However, the more we go into this legislative proposal, and certainly listen to the debate tonight, the more we see, both in terms of content and in terms of good legislation, which I can imagine you feel extra responsible for. That is what we are going to consider now, but not after I have said it again, I have just said it, and I appreciate it, not only for the persistence, but also for the passion with which the initiators talk about childcare. Mr. Chairman. The cabinet has taken a neutral position on the initiative proposal. In doing so, we take into account the political-administrative relations in our new governance culture. From a legislative proposal that has now reached this Chamber, we see the debate in this Chamber as much as possible, and we, as a cabinet in our role as advisor, would like to make a number of comments and comments on the examination of this initiative proposal. That is an answer to Mrs De Bruijn-Wezeman. As a cabinet, we consulted various documents, including, like you, the advice and information of the Council of State and the opinion of the Vermeij Committee. I will explain the cabinet's position, partly on the basis of these documents. I would also like to touch on a number of implementation issues. Coherence with COOVID-19 will of course also be discussed. First of all, the Council of State's opinion on the initiative proposal. Many of you have referred to this, including the critical remarks made by the Council of State. In particular, the Council of State wonders whether the real solution is not to increase the vaccination rate. We agree with that. Moreover, the Council of State states that childcare organisations do not need an explicit legal basis for refusing children who do not participate in the RVP, because, as many of you have said, childcare organisations can already pursue policies that allow them to refuse children and employees who do not participate in the National Vaccination Programme. After all, they are private companies. In doing so, they are bound by the General Law on Equal Treatment, by the privacy law, so when they refuse children or employees, it may conflict with the right to freedom of religion or belief. This means, in the current situation, that the childcare organisation itself must be able to objectively justify the indirect distinction. The initiative proposal provides for a legal exception to this prohibition in the General Law on Equal Treatment. In this way, the initiators aim to create legal certainty for childcare organisations. In the eyes of the initiators, the law provides that objective justification and is no longer for individual childcare organisations. Now the Council of State is giving you a statement that you have just had a debate on the issue of the need to deal with the introduction of such legal exceptions to prohibitions under the General Law on equal treatment. If an exception is to be made, the interests must be carefully weighed. This is the only way to justify that indirect distinction. For this, it is also important to mention that the proposal pursues a legitimate objective. In the view of the Council of State, an objective that aims at providing transparency and parents' concerns is insufficient to create these legal exceptions. In the opinion of the Council of State, the initiative proposal can only be justified in the interests of public health. Many of you referred to this and we endorse it. In response to the information provided by the Council of State, the initiators indicate that they are in fact balancing interests in the parliamentary documents. They also indicate that they added public health protection as a secondary objective to the bill. The cabinet still considers that the balance of interests made in those parliamentary documents is insufficient to objectively justify that indirect distinction. I explain this by going deeper into the two objectives that the initiators pursue. The first objective, the primary objective, is transparency for both parents and holders of children's centres. The initiative proposal makes it possible for parents to find out through the National Register for Childcare which childcare organisations have a refusal policy. Parents are thus supposed to be able to make a well informed choice. The initiative proposal provides for transparency of the legal admissibility of a refusal policy for children's centres and thus provides greater legal certainty for holders. However, the initiative proposal only provides for legal certainty and notification to the National Register for Childcare when the holder of a children's centre has a policy of refusal for all vaccinations. For example, the law is laid down in the National Vaccination Programme. So for children's centres which only have a refusal policy for the BMR vaccination, i.e. for mumps, measles and ruffled dogs, this initiative proposal does not provide legal certainty. In these cases, nothing is mentioned in the National Register for Childcare. You have to comply with all the vaccinations. And with that, you only contribute very little to transparency. In addition, there are already childcare organisations that have a policy of refusal for unvaccinated children and for staff. As far as we know, they only have a refusal policy for the BMR vaccination. The question is how many childcare organisations will actually adopt a policy of refusal for the entire National vaccination programme. We are also wondering, as a cabinet, whether this initiative proposal does not make it more unclear for parents. Childcare organisations with a limited refusal policy are therefore not listed in that register. Then there is the secondary objective, the protection of public health. The cabinet lacks here a good basis for the health gains that would result from a policy of refusal. Many of you have referred to this. The initiators argue that the vaccination rate may increase and consider it highly possible that parents who have doubts about vaccination will proceed to have their children still participate in the RVP, the so-called nudging. The cabinet lacks a good basis and therefore questions the effectiveness of these measures on increasing the vaccination rate. Increasing the vaccination rate is ultimately the goal we are going for. Finally, a number of you referred to the fact that there is still a risk of contamination in a childcare facility in a policy of refusal. Children under 14 months of age are not yet vaccinated against bmr. They could develop or transmit these diseases. That was also the point made by the Council of State in its opinion. This could lead to false safety, as Mrs Vos pointed out, because parents are wrongly feeling that their children are safe from infectious diseases in a childcare facility that has a refusal policy. In conclusion, the Cabinet is not convinced that this initiative proposal contributes to the objectives of transparency and public health protection. In our view, the requirement that there should be a necessary means of achieving a legitimate objective is not met. Mr Ganzevoort referred to this on several occasions and asked whether it was true that the reasoning that public health cannot be the purpose of the legislative proposal in a legislative sense is not proven to contribute to public health. That's right. You are right to ask whether we think so. So now I'm gonna skip two pages, Mr President, and I'm gonna move on to the Human Rights College.

ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u518	The College of Human Rights. Your Chamber has put a very clear question to the College of Human Rights. In its reply, the College states that his role has not changed and that he remains competent in concrete situations to assess the refusal policy of childcare organisations. Mr Verkerk and Mr Schalk asked for this. There has been a lot of discussion about it just now. The cabinet endorses the opinion of this college. I shall pass on the further explanation and I will hear it if you have any further questions. Then I'll switch to the Vermeij committee. I think we all recognise the struggle to find a good solution to the vaccination issue. We've had that struggle here tonight. In 2018, our predecessors at Social Affairs and VWS set up the Childcare and Vaccination Committee, the Vermeij Committee, which came up with its advice Grabs. The Vermeij Committee has presented various options for action. In doing so, it indicates that the option of action, as proposed by the initiators, is that legal power for childcare organisations to refuse children who do not participate in the vaccination programme, is not in line with its central principles. First of all, the solution to the problem is put on the label of the sector, while the Vermeij Committee has set out as its starting point that the primary responsibility for taking measures, as a result of a possible fall in vaccination rates, lies with the government and not with childcare. Protection of public health is a public task. As a cabinet, we agree with that. Secondly, like the Council of State, the committee states that providing transparency and parents' concerns are not sufficient grounds for taking legal action. Like the committee, the cabinet feels it is undesirable for the social debate on the reduced vaccination rate to be submitted to childcare organisations with this initiative legislative proposal, as it makes children's centre holders responsible for solving a social problem. Both the AJN Youth Doctors Netherlands and the Branche organisatie Kindervanging also opposed the initiative legislative proposal for similar reasons. They also believe that public health is primarily a public task and that it should remain there. The cabinet agrees with the committee: the only thing that really reduces the risks to children's health is increasing the overall vaccination rate. The Secretary of State will go into this further. Measures other than the initiative proposal are more effective in this respect, for example in the field of information or disinformation. For this reason, we continue to pursue all actions aimed at increasing and sustaining the vaccination rate for the various vaccinations in the RVP. The Secretary of State and I will look together at how we are going to proceed with the opinions of the Vermeij Committee. We will also explicitly consider the role that childcare organisations can play in communicating with parents about vaccination. I will discuss this with industry organisations for childcare. I say to Mrs Gerkens: with attention also to the disadvantaged areas with a low SES. I think you also mentioned Turkish and Moroccan parents. In addition, it is important that the report "Pikken" for each other of the Vermeij Committee makes a distinction between a green, an orange and a red scenario. If the vaccination rate reaches a lower limit to be determined, participation in the RVP is required for access to childcare. In 2019, the previous cabinet indicated that it was in favour of this measure and that it should be further developed. Corona did not do that, but we are going to take that effect up again and see how we intend to follow up on this process. We will inform you about this shortly after the summer.

At the moment, therefore, this concerns staff and children aged between 5 and 12 years, as the initiative proposal affects children's centres and affects extracurricular care for children aged between 5 and 12 years. The Council considers that the choice of parents is whether or not to take advantage of the offer of a COOVID-19 vaccination. This must not lead to exclusion from school or social exclusion from those children. The cabinet has taken the advice of the Health Council. However, this proposal for an initiative will enable children who have not been vaccinated for COVID-19 to be excluded from children's centres, i.e. those who have a policy of refusal. ParlaMint-NL_2022-04-19-eerstekamer-6.u563	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I am very pleased to be standing here with you tonight. This is my first time, my scoop, in the Senate. Then I will say something about vaccination policy. Communication is the third block. Finally, there are the other questions. First of all, what exactly is the situation with the facts and figures? It has already been discussed in the debate, but I would like to make it quite clear. I've got it right here, too. When it comes to the birth cohorts, we did indeed see a falling line until 2016. Then my predecessor wrote a rather alarming letter, further with vaccination, and also made a number of choices to tighten up the vaccination programme, the RVP. That's what happened then. At that time, the proposal for an own-initiative act was also tabled. Now it actually comes together. There really was an urgency, because you saw that falling trend. At that time, we were lucky to see that trend recovered somewhat. In the middle of the covid situation we are indeed seeing a slight deterioration of 1 to 2 percentage points. I would also like to point out that this slight deterioration is indeed a deterioration, and at the same time a relative deterioration in the European perspective. If you look at how the Dutch RVP did it compared to the rest of Europe, fortunately it has been quite good. At the same time, I would also like to say that the figures are below the WHO level of 95%. This therefore calls for a firm and alert attitude towards this programme. So we will also have to continue with the RVP and increase the vaccination rate. That is what I would like to say in a general sense, to indicate what we are doing now. You can continue to fill that out yourself. Then I have one more question in this block about clustering. In this respect, the cabinet initially said, at the time of the first appreciation of the bill, that there might be a cluster risk. The RIVM then said: that may not be the case. The reasoning behind this is that RIVM states that, in particular, the national vaccination rate ultimately determines the risk of an outbreak at a location, and the specific vaccination rate at locations to a lesser extent. So what RIVM is saying about that potentially increased risk of cluster contamination is actually a nuance to the government's first appreciation. Then I go to my second block, namely vaccination policy. I'm just going to answer some of the questions for a second. The VVD had a question about this. She said: the WHO is actually calling for vaccination to be brought to the highest possible level and to actively combat the falling trend. I very much agree with that. I've already turned that in. My predecessor also said about this urgent downward trend: we really need to get to work on this. He introduced the action plan Further with vaccination. I am going to elaborate on this before the summer. I am going to look into how we can also strengthen this action plan with the current facts. There are all kinds of wonderful examples of how to do that best, but for the summer I will come to the House of Representatives, and if you wish of course also to the House of Representatives, with an explanation of how that action plan will be further strengthened. There was also a question about D66, namely: what does the government intend to keep that vaccination rate high? I told you, quite a firm policy has been put in place. There is still a great deal going on in this area, including an invitation to provide additional information, to send repeat calls, to check contact moments and to see if there are still open vaccinations. All this is done within the framework of the 1 or 2 percentage points lower presence around the covid vaccinations of the other National vaccination programme. GroenLinks asked to zoom in on different groups, because that might help to get a little more insight into what helps within which group. I'm just gonna get two groups out of it anyway. That's the vaccination rate of people in the Biblebelt. The second is residents of specific neighbourhoods in the major cities. Interestingly enough I can also share something about both groups from my own experience. I'm not gonna do all of that, but I think I can point it out a little bit. First of all, the Biblebelt. We can see that the jgz has made a number of additional interventions here too. In Barneveld, for example, a standard conversation is offered about vaccinations. The results are actually very positive. Almost all parents visit the consultation. The first experience is that the consultation is highly appreciated, gives a lot of knowledge to parents about vaccination that they did not have and removes incorrect information. This is an example of how we address this particular issue. Another target group is, of course, the people in the specific neighbourhoods in the big cities. What we're doing there is basically working with key figures. Neighborhoods in The Hague and Utrecht have made very specific and targeted choices: extra time for professionals to engage in conversation with parents, after calling parents, because very often it is not an active, but an inactive choice, and sometimes also on the spot vaccinate during home visits. I would therefore like to reply in particular: it is still a very urgent matter to be constantly working to increase the vaccination rate. He is still not at the safe level, at the 95% of the WHO. In short, there is still work to be done. But fortunately, the declining trend has been slightly reversed. That was badly needed, otherwise we might have come under 90%. That is ultimately the factor that leads to the most security. That is what the cabinet is committed to as much as possible. Then I move on to the third block, namely the vaccination communication.

"Thereby justified," so by checking it against the law. If we include that exception in the law, then that will always lead to an award, unless it is contrary to the law. The College therefore states that a legal exception as proposed in this bill does not affect the power of the College to hear a dispute. We have just agreed on this, but I am sure that I will say it again here. The College is not competent if, for example, there is no legal grounds for non-discrimination or if a complaint is made outside the areas in which the College is legally competent. If a dispute in which the college is competent to take note of that college is referred to, the college will only check whether the admission policy of the child centre is covered by the exception to the prohibition of discrimination. Let me be clear: that exception to the prohibition of discrimination is introduced with this bill. If the child centre applies an admission policy which is covered by that exception to the ban and therefore if a children's centre complies neatly with the law, then the indirect distinction, I quote the college again, is justified.
